The '''2020 United States Senate elections''' were held on November 3, 2020,<ref name="8BVmB"/> with the 33 [[Classes of United States senators#Class 2|class 2]] seats of the [[U.S. Senate|Senate]] contested in regular elections.<ref name="GQmmU"/> Of these, 21 were held by [[Republican Party (United States)|Republicans]], and 12 by [[Democratic Party (United States)|Democrats]]. The winners were elected to 6-year terms from January 3, 2021, to January 3, 2027.<ref name="mG5zT"/> Two special elections for seats held by Republicans were also held in conjunction with the general elections: one in [[2020 United States Senate special election in Arizona|Arizona]], to fill the vacancy created by [[John McCain]]'s death in 2018; and one in [[2020–21 United States Senate special election in Georgia|Georgia]], following [[Johnny Isakson]]'s resignation in 2019.<ref name="JkEBT"/><ref name="IwJ0O"/><ref name="Foran 2020"/><ref name="Georgia 2021"/> These elections ran concurrently with the [[2020 United States presidential election]] in which incumbent president [[Donald Trump]] lost to Democratic nominee [[Joe Biden]].

In the [[2014 United States Senate elections]], the last regularly scheduled elections for Class 2 Senate seats, the Republicans won nine seats from the Democrats and gained a majority,<ref name="3LNTO"/> which they continued to hold after the [[2016 United States Senate elections|2016]] and [[2018 United States Senate elections|2018]] elections.<ref name="CHgH7"/><ref>{{cite news|last=Foran|first=Clare|date=November 7, 2018|url=https://edition.cnn.com/2018/11/06/politics/senate-control-midterm-elections/index.html|title=Republicans keep control of Senate in 2018 mid-terms, CNN projects|agency=CNN|access-date=January 10, 2021}}</ref> Before the elections, Republicans held 53 seats, Democrats held 45 seats, and independents caucusing with the Democrats held two seats, neither of which were up for re-election.<ref name="hTe6z"/> Including the special elections in Arizona and Georgia, Republicans defended 23 seats, and the Democrats defended 12.<ref name="rollingstone-battle"/>

In this election, the Democratic Party made a net gain of three Senate seats and the vice presidency, giving them a majority for the first time since 2014, albeit by a narrow 50–50 margin.{{efn|name=majority}}<ref name="Stevens 2020"/> Democrats unseated four Republicans in [[2020 United States Senate special election in Arizona|Arizona]], [[2020 United States Senate election in Colorado|Colorado]], and both elections in Georgia – while Republicans flipped a seat in [[2020 United States Senate election in Alabama|Alabama]]; however, Democrats under-performed expectations overall; despite record-breaking turnout and fund-raising efforts, they failed to flip several seats that were considered competitive, such as [[2020 United States Senate election in North Carolina|North Carolina]], [[2020 United States Senate election in Iowa|Iowa]], and [[2020 United States Senate election in Maine|Maine]], and lost many races by much larger margins than expected.<ref name="Foran 2020">{{cite news |last=Foran |first=Clare |date=November 6, 2020 |title=Mark Kelly defeats Arizona GOP Sen. Martha McSally in key pickup for Democrats |agency=CNN |url=https://www.cnn.com/2020/11/06/politics/mark-kelly-arizona-senate/index.html |access-date=November 13, 2020}}</ref><ref>{{cite news |last1=Raju |first1=Manu |last2=Rogers |first2=Alex |date=November 4, 2020 |title=Frustrated Democrats eye two more years in minority after falling short in key Senate races |agency=CNN |url=https://www.cnn.com/2020/11/04/politics/senate-election-results-frustrated-democrats/index.html |access-date=November 13, 2020}}</ref> Except in [[2020 United States Senate election in Maine|Maine]], the winning party in every Senate election was the winning party in the state's presidential election.<ref>{{cite news |last=Rakich |first=Nathaniel |date=December 2, 2020 |title=There Wasn't That Much Split-Ticket Voting In 2020 |website=FiveThirtyEight |url=https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/there-wasnt-that-much-split-ticket-voting-in-2020/ |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20201202110219/https://fivethirtyeight.com/features/there-wasnt-that-much-split-ticket-voting-in-2020/ |url-status=dead |archive-date=December 2, 2020 |access-date=January 8, 2021}}</ref>

Due to election laws in Georgia that require candidates to win at least 50% of the vote in the general election, the state's [[2020–21 United States Senate election in Georgia|regularly scheduled]] and [[2020–21 United States Senate special election in Georgia|special]] Senate elections were decided in run-off elections on January 5, 2021.<ref>{{cite news |last=Grisales |first=Claudia |date=November 7, 2020 |title=Senate Control Likely Decided By Fate Of 2 Georgia Run-Off Races |agency=NPR |url=https://www.npr.org/2020/11/07/932068951/senate-control-likely-decided-by-fate-of-2-georgia-runoff-races |access-date=November 8, 2020}}</ref> After the November general election, Republicans held 50 seats, while Democrats held 48 and the vice presidency, so sweeping both races was crucial for Democrats to attain a majority. They succeeded in doing so,<ref name="Georgia 2021">{{cite news|title=Georgia Highlights: Democrats Capture the Senate as Ossoff Defeats Perdue |url=https://www.nytimes.com/live/2021/01/06/us/georgia-election-results |access-date=January 10, 2021 |newspaper=The New York Times|orig-date=January 6, 2021 |date=January 8, 2021 |last1=Martin |first1=Jonathan |last2=Fausset |first2=Richard |last3=Epstein |first3=Reid J. }}</ref> and the partisan balance in the Senate became tied for the third time in history, after the results in the 1880 elections and the 2000 elections.<ref>{{cite web|last=Jacobson|first=Louis|date=January 7, 2021|url=https://www.politifact.com/article/2021/jan/07/how-will-senate-work-under-50-50-tie/|title=How will the Senate work under a 50-50 split?|website=PolitiFact|access-date=January 10, 2021}}</ref><ref>{{cite news|url=https://www.pewresearch.org/fact-tank/2020/12/01/slim-majorities-have-become-more-common-in-the-u-s-senate-and-house/|title=Slim majorities have become more common in the U.S. Senate and House|publisher=Pew Research Center|last=Schaeffer|first=Katherine|date=December 1, 2020|accessdate=April 4, 2020}}</ref> Vice President Kamala Harris's tie-breaking vote gave Democrats control of the chamber by the smallest margin possible after the new administration took office.<ref>{{cite news|last1=Snell|first1=Kelsey|last2=Walsh|first2=Deirdre|url=https://www.npr.org/2021/01/06/953712195/democrats-move-closer-to-senate-control-as-counting-continues-in-georgia?t=1610299162544|title=Democrats Take Control Of Senate With Twin Georgia Victories|agency=NPR|access-date=January 10, 2021}}</ref>

This marked the first time since [[1980 United States elections|1980]] that either chamber of Congress flipped partisan control in a presidential election year, and the first time Democrats did so since [[1948 United States elections|1948]].<ref>{{cite news|url=https://www.washingtonpost.com/politics/2021/01/06/trump-set-be-first-president-since-1932-lose-reelection-house-senate/|title=Trump set to be first president since 1932 to lose re-election, the House, and the Senate|newspaper=[[The Washington Post]]|last=Blake|first=Aaron|date=January 6, 2021|accessdate=January 6, 2021}}</ref> This was the fourth presidential election cycle in a row, after 2008, 2012 and 2016, where Democrats made gains.

== Change in composition ==
Republicans defended 23 seats, while Democrats defended 12.<ref name="rollingstone-battle" /> Each block represents one of the 100 Senate seats. "D#" is a Democratic senator, "I#" is an independent senator, and "R#" is a Republican senator. They are arranged so that the parties are separated, and a majority is clear by crossing the middle.

=== Before the elections ===
Each block indicates an incumbent senator's actions going into the election. Both Independents caucus with the Democrats.

== Final pre-election predictions ==
Several sites and individuals published predictions of competitive seats. These predictions looked at factors such as the strength of the [[incumbent]] (if the incumbent was running for re-election) and the other candidates, and the state's partisan lean (reflected in part by the state's [[Cook Partisan Voting Index]] rating). The predictions assigned ratings to each seat, indicating the predicted advantage that a party had in winning that seat. Most election predictors used:
* "<u>tossup</u>": no advantage
* "<u>tilt</u>" (used by some predictors): advantage that is not quite as strong as "lean"
* "<u>lean</u>": slight advantage
* "<u>likely</u>": significant, but surmountable, advantage
* "<u>safe</u>" or "<u>solid</u>": near-certain chance of victory<!-- Colors and sorting:

In many cases, the incumbent has yet to say their intention of running for another term. If an incumbent intends to retire then place "<br />{{Small|(Retiring)}}" after the incumbent's name.

See [[Template:USRaceRating]] for how to apply rating.

-->

===Post-election changes===
One Democrat resigned shortly after the start of the 117th Congress and was replaced by a Democratic appointee. In [[2022 United States Senate elections in California|California]], a regular scheduled election was held in the [[2022 United States Senate elections|2022 Senate elections]] for the Class 3 terms, where Democrat appointee Alex Padilla won the election.

== Race summary ==
=== Special elections during the preceding Congress ===
In each special election, the winner's term begins immediately after their election is certified by their state's government.

Elections are sorted by date then state.

== Alabama ==
{{Infobox election

| country = Alabama

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Alabama}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Alabama|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Alabama}}

Incumbent Democrat [[Doug Jones (politician)|Doug Jones]] was elected in a special election [[2017 United States Senate special election in Alabama|in 2017]], narrowly defeating Republican nominee [[Roy Moore]].<ref name="BAJYc" /><ref name="qqHQg" /> He ran for a full term in 2020, losing to Republican [[Tommy Tuberville]] in a landslide.

Tuberville is a former football head coach for [[Auburn University]]. He defeated former senator and [[U.S. Attorney General|attorney general]] [[Jeff Sessions]] in a July 14 run-off to secure the Republican nomination, after securing President Donald Trump's endorsement. Sessions occupied the seat until 2017 when he resigned to become attorney general in the Trump administration.

Alabama is one of the country's most Republican states, and Jones's win was in part due to sexual assault allegations against nominee [[Roy Moore]] during the special election; most analysts expected the seat to flip back to GOP control. Tuberville defeated Jones by more than 20 percentage points.<ref name="Alabama competitive" />

== Alaska ==

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Alaska}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Alaska|2020 United States House of Representatives election in Alaska}}

One-term Republican [[Dan Sullivan (politician, born 1964)|Dan Sullivan]] was elected in 2014, defeating incumbent Democrat [[Mark Begich]]. He defeated independent challenger [[Al Gross (politician)|Al Gross]] to win a second term in office.<ref name="uPWzo" />

Potential Democratic candidates included Begich, who was the Democratic nominee for [[governor of Alaska]] in [[2018 Alaska gubernatorial election|2018]], and [[Mayor of Anchorage|Anchorage mayor]] [[Ethan Berkowitz]], who was the Democratic nominee for [[governor of Alaska]] in [[2010 Alaska gubernatorial election|2010]]. One Democrat, [[Edgar Blatchford]], filed to run by the June 1 filing deadline.<ref name="AK2020" />

Gross, an orthopedic surgeon and fisherman, declared his candidacy on July 2, 2019, as an independent.<ref name="r3sJv" /> He participated in a joint primary for the [[Alaska Democratic Party]], [[Alaska Libertarian Party]] and [[Alaskan Independence Party]], winning the nomination as an independent supported by the Democratic Party.

Despite predictions of a close race, Sullivan defeated Gross by 12.7 percentage points.<ref>Axelrod, Tal & Zack Budryk. [https://www.msn.com/en-us/news/politics/sullivan-wins-reelection-in-alaska-senate-race/ar-BB1aUPHF Sullivan wins re-election in Alaska, giving Republicans 50 seats in Senate], ''[[The Hill (newspaper)|The Hill]]'', November 11, 2020.</ref>

{{Main|2020 United States Senate special election in Arizona}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Arizona|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Arizona}}
Six-term Republican [[John McCain]] was re-elected in 2016, but died in office on August 25, 2018, after a battle with brain cancer.<ref name="5knVe" /> Republican governor [[Doug Ducey]] appointed former senator [[Jon Kyl]] to fill the seat temporarily.<ref name="7znU0" /> After Kyl resigned at the end of the year, Ducey appointed outgoing U.S. Representative [[Martha McSally]] to replace him after she lost the election to the other Arizona senate seat.<ref name="De705" /> McSally ran in the 2020 special election to fill the remaining two years of the term,<ref name="shepardaz1" /> losing to Democrat [[Mark Kelly]], a former astronaut.

Once a solidly Republican state, Arizona trended more [[Purple state|purple]] in the late 2010s. Incumbent Republican [[Martha McSally]] was appointed to McCain's seat two months after losing the [[2018 United States Senate election in Arizona|2018 Arizona U.S. Senate election]] to Democrat [[Kyrsten Sinema]]. Her Democratic opponent, astronaut [[Mark Kelly]], raised significantly more money and generally led her by 5 to 15 points in the polling. McSally also suffered from low approval ratings due to her strong allegiance to Trump, who was unpopular in Arizona despite having won the state by 3.5 points in 2016.<ref name="Arizona competitive" />

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Arkansas}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Arkansas|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Arkansas}}

One-term Republican [[Tom Cotton]] was elected in 2014, after serving two years in the [[United States House of Representatives]], defeating incumbent Democratic senator [[Mark Pryor]] by a comfortable margin. Cotton was re-elected to a second term by a 33-point margin, defeating [[Libertarian Party (United States)|Libertarian]] Ricky Dale Harrington Jr.<ref name="cottonrunning" /><ref>{{Cite web |title=2020 Arkansas Senate Election Results |url=https://www.realclearpolitics.com/elections/live_results/2020/state/ar/senate/#! |access-date=December 15, 2020 |website=Real Clear Politics}}</ref><ref name="RHarrington" />

Joshua Mahony, a non-profit executive and 2018 Democratic nominee for Congress in [[Arkansas's 3rd congressional district]], filed to run for the Democratic nomination,<ref name="60NPs" /> but dropped out just after the filing deadline.<ref name="ZWBkr" /> No other Democrats filed within the filing deadline. Progressive activist Dan Whitfield ran as an independent, but suspended his campaign on October 1, 2020, after failing to qualify for the ballot.<ref name="Whitfield-AR" />

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Colorado}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Colorado|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Colorado}}

One-term Republican [[Cory Gardner]] was elected in 2014 after serving four years in the [[United States House of Representatives]], narrowly defeating one-term Democrat [[Mark Udall]]. Gardner sought a second term but lost to Democrat John Hickenlooper by 9.3 percentage points.<ref name="rothco1" />

[[John Hickenlooper|Hickenlooper]] is a popular former [[governor of Colorado]], and led Gardner by as much as 20 percentage points in polls, with most pundits considering him a heavy favorite. Gardner was Colorado's only Republican statewide officeholder, and the once purple state has trended increasingly Democratic since his narrow win in [[2014 United States Senate election in Colorado|2014]]. Gardner also had low approval ratings due to his strong allegiance to Trump, who lost Colorado in 2016 to [[Hillary Clinton]] by 4.9%, and in 2020 to [[Joe Biden]] by 13.5%.<ref name="QJHOC" /><ref name="9Qtfg" /> Hickenlooper also raised significantly more money than Gardner.<ref name="Colorado competitive" />

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Delaware}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Delaware|2020 United States House of Representatives election in Delaware}}

One-term Democrat [[Chris Coons]] was re-elected in 2014; he first took office after winning a [[2010 United States Senate special election in Delaware|2010 special election]], which occurred after long-time senator [[Joe Biden]] resigned to become [[Vice President of the United States|vice president of the United States]] (Biden also won the [[2020 United States presidential election|2020 presidential election]] and became president). He faced an unsuccessful primary challenge from technology executive Jessica Scarane. Conservative activist [[Lauren Witzke]] and attorney Jim DeMartino ran for the Republican nomination.

The Delaware primary was held on September 15, 2020.<ref name="Sy7FH" />

== Georgia ==
{{See also|List of United States senators from Georgia|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Georgia}}

Due to Republican senator [[Johnny Isakson]]'s resignation from office for health reasons in 2019, both of Georgia's Senate seats were up for election in November 2020.<ref>{{Cite web |author1=Alex Rogers|author2=Eric Bradner|author3=Kaitlan Collins|author4=Phil Mattingly |date=August 28, 2019 |title=Georgia Republican Sen. Johnny Isakson to resign at end of year |url=https://www.cnn.com/2019/08/28/politics/johnny-isakson-retire/index.html |url-status=live |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210107183318/http://www.cnn.com/2019/08/28/politics/johnny-isakson-retire/index.html |archive-date=January 7, 2021 |access-date=January 8, 2021 |website=CNN Digital |language=en}}</ref> The state had tilted Republican in Senate races since the mid-1990s, but increased support for Democrats in populous suburbs has made office elections more competitive; a close governor's race, multiple close U.S. House races, and many other close local office races resulted in Democratic gains in [[2018 Georgia state elections|2018]] elections. Both the regular and special election were considered highly competitive toss-ups.<ref>{{Cite web |date=October 29, 2020 |title=2020 Senate Race ratings |url=https://cookpolitical.com/ratings/senate-race-ratings |url-status=live |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210105155133/https://cookpolitical.com/ratings/senate-race-ratings |archive-date=January 5, 2021 |access-date=January 8, 2021 |website=The Cook Political Report |language=en}}</ref> Both of these elections received national attention, as if Republicans won at least one of these seats, they would maintain a Senate majority, but if the Democrats won both, the Senate would be split 50/50 with Vice President [[Kamala Harris]] breaking the tie.

}}{{Main|2020–21 United States Senate election in Georgia}}
One-term Republican [[David Perdue]] was elected in 2014, and sought a second term.<ref name="perdue2020" />

[[Jon Ossoff]], a former [[2017 Georgia's 6th congressional district special election|congressional candidate]], [[documentary film]] producer, and investigative journalist, defeated former [[Columbus, Georgia|Columbus]] [[mayor]] [[Teresa Tomlinson]] and [[2018 Georgia lieutenant gubernatorial election|2018 lieutenant governor nominee]] [[Sarah Riggs Amico]] in the Democratic primary to secure nomination.<ref name="8LzFW" /><ref name="qKzrl" /> He faced incumbent Republican [[David Perdue]] in the November 3 election.

In the November election, no candidate received 50% or more of the total vote; per Georgia law, the election advanced to a [[Two-round system|run-off]] between the top two finishers, Ossoff and Perdue, on January 5, 2021. Ossoff was projected the winner on January 6,<ref>{{Cite web |date=January 6, 2021 |title=Ossoff wins in Georgia, tipping Senate control to Democrats |url=https://www.pbs.org/newshour/politics/ossoff-wins-in-georgia-tipping-senate-control-to-democrats |access-date=January 6, 2021 |website=PBS NewsHour}}</ref> and Perdue conceded on January 8.<ref>{{Cite news |date=January 8, 2021 |title=Republican Perdue concedes Georgia U.S. Senate race to Democrat |work=Reuters |url=https://www.reuters.com/article/us-usa-election-georgia-idUSKBN29D2SM |access-date=January 9, 2021}}</ref>

{{Main|2020–21 United States Senate special election in Georgia}}
Three-term senator [[Johnny Isakson]] announced on August 28, 2019, that he would resign from the Senate on December 31, 2019, citing health concerns.<ref name="isaksonretiring" /> Georgia governor [[Brian Kemp]] [[List of appointed United States senators|appointed]] Republican [[Kelly Loeffler]] to replace Isakson until a special election could be held; Loeffler took office on January 6, 2020, and competed in the November 2020 election to retain her seat.<ref name="PviCd" />

Other Republicans who ran for the seat included Wayne Johnson, former chief operating officer of the [[Office of Federal Student Aid]],<ref name="ni6IX" /> and four-term U.S. representative [[Doug Collins (politician)|Doug Collins]].<ref name="auto1" />

A "[[jungle primary]]" was held November 3, 2020, but no candidate won more than 50% of the vote, so a [[runoff election|run-off election]] between the top two finishers, Loeffler and Democratic challenger [[Raphael Warnock]], was held on January 5, 2021.<ref name="ZYYZN" /> Warnock defeated Loeffler, who initially refused to concede and vowed to challenge the outcome,<ref>{{Cite news |last1=Peoples |first1=Steve |last2=Barrow |first2=Bill |last3=Bynum |first3=Russ |date=January 7, 2021 |title=Warnock makes history with Senate win as Dems near majority |publisher=The Associated Press |url=https://apnews.com/article/Georgia-election-results-4b82ba7ee3cc74d33e68daadaee2cbf3 |access-date=January 6, 2021}}</ref> but conceded on January 7, after the [[2021 storming of the United States Capitol|storming of the U.S. Capitol]].<ref>{{Cite web |last=Nadler |first=Ben |date=January 7, 2021 |title=GOP's Loeffler concedes to Warnock in Georgia run-off |url=https://apnews.com/article/joe-biden-johnny-isakson-georgia-media-jon-ossoff-5b120983d3f1e6adf9b0efe46a265ce8 |url-status=live |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210108025739/https://apnews.com/article/joe-biden-johnny-isakson-georgia-media-jon-ossoff-5b120983d3f1e6adf9b0efe46a265ce8 |archive-date=January 8, 2021 |access-date=January 8, 2021 |website=AP NEWS}}</ref>

Two-term republican [[Jim Risch]] successfully ran for a third term in 2020, defeating Democrat [[Paulette Jordan]] in a landslide. Jordan is a former gubernatorial nominee and former [[Coeur d'Alene people|Coeur d'Alene]] Tribal Councilwoman.

| title = U.S. senator

| before_party = Democratic Party (US)

| after_party = Democratic Party (US)
| election_date = 
}}
{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Illinois}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Illinois|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Illinois}}
Four-term democrat  and [[Assistant party leaders of the United States Senate|Senate minority whip]] [[Dick Durbin]], easily won a fifth term in office, defeating Republican [[Mark Curran]] by a 16-point margin.<ref name="DDurbin" />

Curran served as [[sheriff]] of [[Lake County, Illinois|Lake County]] from 2006 to 2018 and won the Republican primary with 41.55% of the vote.<ref name="XlgL2" />

Antiwar activist Marilyn Jordan Lawlor<ref name="MJLawlor" /> and [[Illinois House of Representatives|state representative]] [[Anne Stava-Murray]]<ref name="J3t1W" /> briefly challenged Durbin in the Democratic primary, but both ended up withdrawing.<ref name="T8pRc" /><ref name="GDsCt" />

[[2019 Chicago mayoral election|2019 Chicago mayoral candidate]] [[Willie Wilson (businessman)|Willie Wilson]], a businessman and [[perennial candidate]], ran as a member of the "Willie Wilson Party," with the backing of a handful of Chicago aldermen and the Chicago Police Union.

 <!-- Election date not needed if General AND same as main article's date-->| image_size = x136px

| after_party = Republican Party (US)
| election_date = 
}}
{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Iowa}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Iowa|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Iowa}}

One-term republican [[Joni Ernst]], first elected to the Senate in 2014, won a second term in office, defeating Democrat [[Theresa Greenfield]].<ref name="s9osH" />

Greenfield won the Democratic nomination, defeating former vice-admiral [[Michael T. Franken]], attorney Kimberly Graham, and businessman Eddie Mauro in the primary.

Ernst's popularity had dropped in polls, and many considered this seat a possible Democratic pick-up, but Ernst was re-elected by a larger-than-expected 6.5 points.

== Kansas ==
{{Infobox election

| after_party = Republican Party (US)
| election_date = 
}}
{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Kansas}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Kansas|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Kansas}}

Four-term  Republican [[Pat Roberts]], was re-elected in 2014 with 53.15% of the vote, and announced on January 4, 2019, that he would not be running for re-election in 2020.

In the Republican primary, United States representative [[Roger Marshall]] defeated former [[Secretary of State of Kansas|Kansas secretary of state]] [[Kris Kobach]],<ref name="3QA9L" /> [[Kansas Turnpike Authority|state Turnpike Authority]] chairman [[Dave Lindstrom]],<ref name="94QuG" /> [[Kansas Senate|state senate]] president [[Susan Wagle]], and others.<ref name="vuF3W" />

There was considerable speculation about a Senate bid by [[Mike Pompeo]] (the [[United States Secretary of State|United States secretary of state]], former [[director of the Central Intelligence Agency]], and former U.S. representative for [[Kansas's 4th congressional district]]), but he did not run.<ref name="KbLGj" /><ref name="Hf989" />

[[Barbara Bollier]], a [[Kansas State Senate|state senator]] and former Republican,<ref name="fj2s5" /> defeated former congressional candidate Robert Tillman<ref name="RTillman" /> for the Democratic nomination, but lost to Marshall with a more than expected 11.4 point margin.

== Kentucky ==
{{Infobox election
| election_name = Kentucky election
| country = Kentucky
| type = presidential
| ongoing = no

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Kentucky}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Kentucky|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Kentucky}}

[[Republican Party (United States)|Republican]] [[Mitch McConnell]], the [[Party leaders of the United States Senate|Senate Majority Leader]], defeated Democrat [[Amy McGrath]] by 19.6 percentage points, winning a 7th term in office. This was his largest margin of victory since [[2002 United States Senate election in Kentucky|2002]].

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Louisiana}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Louisiana|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Louisiana}}

Republican [[Bill Cassidy]] won a second term in office, defeating Democrat [[Adrian Perkins]] and others.<ref name="cUQGY" />

A [[Louisiana primary]] (a form of [[jungle primary]]) was held on November 3. Had no candidate won a majority of the vote in the primary, a [[runoff election|run-off election]] would have been held, but Cassidy won in the first round.

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Maine}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Maine|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Maine}}

Republican [[Susan Collins]] won a fifth term in office, defeating Speaker of the [[Maine House of Representatives]] [[Sara Gideon]].<ref name="Kb2tz" />

Gideon consistently led Collins in polls for almost the entire election cycle. Collins is considered one of the most moderate Republicans in the Senate and had never faced a competitive re-election campaign, even though Maine leans Democratic. But she faced growing unpopularity due to her increasingly conservative voting record, and her votes to confirm [[Brett Kavanaugh]] to the Supreme Court and to acquit Trump in his impeachment trial. Despite almost all polling and Gideon's formidable funding,<ref name="Maine competitive" /> Collins was re-elected by a surprising 8.6-point margin.

Educator and activist Lisa Savage also ran as a candidate for the Green party.<ref name="XSZQe" />

 | map_caption        = <br>'''Markey:''' {{legend0|#A5B0FF|40–50%}} {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#584cde|70–80%}} {{legend0|#3933e5|80–90%}} {{legend0|#0D0596|>90%}}<br/>'''O'Connor''': {{legend0|#ffb2b2|40–50%}} {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}}<br/>'''Tie''': {{legend0|#d2b1d9|40–50%}}

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Massachusetts}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Massachusetts|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Massachusetts}}

Democrat [[Ed Markey]] was re-elected in 2014, having won a 2013 special election to replace long-time incumbent [[John Kerry]], who resigned to become [[U.S. Secretary of State|U.S. secretary of state]]. He easily won a second full term in 2020, defeating Republican Kevin O'Connor by more than 33 percentage points.<ref name="Q4FkV" />

Markey fended off a primary challenge from [[Joe Kennedy III]], four-term U.S. representative for Massachusetts's Fourth District and grandson of former U.S. senator and U.S. attorney general [[Robert F. Kennedy]]. This marked the first time a member of the Kennedy family lost an election in Massachusetts.<ref name="Q3bb5" />

O'Connor defeated [[Shiva Ayyadurai]], a former independent senate candidate, in the Republican primary.<ref name="RanAG" /><ref name="Xx8KY" />

On August 24, 2020, perennial candidate [[Vermin Supreme]] launched a write-in campaign for the Libertarian nomination,<ref name="MAsupreme" /> but received too few votes to qualify for the general election ballot.<ref name="SXk45" />

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Michigan}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Michigan|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Michigan}}

Democrat [[Gary Peters]] narrowly won a second term in office,<ref name="ujOMn" /> defeating Republican [[John E. James|John James]].

James won a Republican Michigan Senate nomination for his second time,<ref name="uh7S8" /> having run against incumbent Democrat [[Debbie Stabenow]] in [[2018 United States Senate election in Michigan|2018]] for Michigan's other senate seat. He faced only token opposition for the 2020 Republican nomination, running against perennial candidate Bob Carr.<ref name="55frb" />

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Minnesota}}

Incumbent Democrat [[Tina Smith]] was appointed to the [[U.S. Senate]] to replace [[Al Franken]] in 2018 after serving as [[Lieutenant Governor of Minnesota|lieutenant governor]], and won a special election later in 2018 to serve the remainder of Franken's term. She defeated Republican [[Jason Lewis (Minnesota politician)|Jason Lewis]], winning her first full term in office.<ref name="uATUe" />

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Mississippi}}

Incumbent Republican [[Cindy Hyde-Smith]] won her first full term in office, defeating Democrat and former [[United States Secretary of Agriculture|U.S. secretary of agriculture]] [[Mike Espy]] by 10 percentage points.<ref name="DU7pa" /> This race was an exact rematch of the [[2018 United States Senate special election in Mississippi|2018 Mississippi Senate special election]], in which Hyde-Smith defeated Espy for the remaining two years of the seat's term.

Libertarian candidate Jimmy Edwards also made the general election ballot.

== Montana ==
{{Infobox election

 | country            = Montana

 <!-- Election date not needed if General AND same as main article's date-->
 | image_size         = x136px
 | image1             = Steve Daines, Official Portrait, 116th Congress.jpg
 | nominee1           = '''[[Steve Daines]]'''
 | party1             = Republican Party (US)
 | popular_vote1      = '''333,174'''
 | percentage1        = '''55.01%'''
 | image2             = Steve Bullock by Gage Skidmore.jpg
 | nominee2           = [[Steve Bullock (American politician)|Steve Bullock]]
 | party2             = Democratic Party (US)
 | popular_vote2      = 272,463
 | percentage2        = 44.99%
 | map_image          = 2020 United States Senate election in Montana results map by county.svg
 | map_size           = 300px
 | map_caption        = County results<br>'''Daines:''' {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}} {{legend0|#c21b18|80–90%}} {{legend0|#a80000|>90%}}<br />'''Bullock:''' {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#584cde|70–80%}}
 | title              = U.S. senator
 | before_election    = [[Steve Daines]]
 | before_party       = Republican Party (US)
 | after_election    = [[Steve Daines]]
 | after_party       = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Montana}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Montana|2020 United States House of Representatives election in Montana}}

Republican [[Steve Daines]] won a second term in office, defeating the Democratic nominee, Montana Governor [[Steve Bullock (American politician)|Steve Bullock]].<ref name="helenair.com" />

Daines was opposed (before his nomination) in the Republican primary by hardware store manager Daniel Larson and former Democratic speaker of the Montana House of Representatives [[John Driscoll (Montana politician)|John Driscoll]], who changed parties in 2020.<ref name="9TRtQ" />

Bullock won the Democratic nomination,<ref name="3UGQT" /> defeating nuclear engineer and U.S. Navy veteran John Mues.<ref name="auto7" />

Libertarian and Green party candidates were set to appear on the general election ballot, but the Libertarians refused to nominate a replacement after their nominee withdrew and the Greens' nominee was disqualified.

Once Bullock filed his candidacy, the race became seen as highly competitive. Bullock, a popular governor and a moderate, led in many polls in the spring and summer of 2020, and raised more money than Daines. Closer to election day, Bullock slightly trailed in polls, but the election was still seen as relatively competitive. Daines defeated Bullock by a larger-than-expected 10-point margin.

== Nebraska ==
{{Infobox election

| map_caption = County results<br>'''Sasse:''' {{legend0|#E27F7F|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#D72F30|70–80%}} {{legend0|#C21B18|80–90%}}
| title = U.S. Senator
| before_election = [[Ben Sasse]]
| before_party = Republican Party (United States)
| after_election = [[Ben Sasse]]
| after_party = Republican Party (United States)
| map_size = 250px
}}
{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Nebraska}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Nebraska|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Nebraska}}

Republican [[Ben Sasse]] easily won a second term in office, defeating Democrat Chris Janicek by more than 30 percentage points.<ref name="zYEwR" />

Sasse had defeated businessman and former [[Lancaster County, Nebraska|Lancaster County]] Republican Party chair Matt Innis in the Republican primary with 75.2% of the vote.

Businessman and [[2018 United States Senate election in Nebraska|2018]] U.S. Senate candidate Chris Janicek won the Democratic primary with 30.7% of the vote, defeating six other candidates.

Libertarian candidate Gene Siadek also appeared on the general election ballot.

After the primary election, the Nebraska Democratic party withdrew its support from Janicek when allegations that he sexually harassed a campaign staffer emerged.<ref name="qvtpo" /> Janicek refused to leave the race despite the state party endorsing his former primary opponent,{{Clarify|reason=|date=January 2021}} which led former Democratic Congressman [[Brad Ashford]] to announce a write-in campaign on August 23, 2020.<ref name="UxBHl" /><ref name="tJH6R" /> After Janicek vowed to remain in the race anyway, Ashford withdrew on August 27, citing lack of time and resources necessary for a U.S. Senate campaign.<ref name="rI5Nh" /> The state Democratic Party subsequently threw its support behind long-time Nebraska activist Preston Love Jr., who declared a write-in candidacy for the seat.<ref name="prestonlove" /><ref name="jTZLe" />

== New Hampshire ==
{{Infobox election
 | election_name      = New Hampshire election

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in New Hampshire}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from New Hampshire|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in New Hampshire}}

Two-term Democrat [[Jeanne Shaheen]] won a third term in office by nearly 16 percentage points, defeating Republican Corky Messner.<ref name="PB3Yf" />

Messner defeated [[U.S. Army]] [[Brigadier general (United States)|brigadier general]] [[Donald C. Bolduc]] and perennial candidate [[Andy Martin]] for the Republican nomination,<ref name="tvQQl" /><ref name="uVdyL" /><ref name="fVNy5" /> winning the nomination on September 8.

Libertarian Justin O'Donnell also appeared on the general election ballot.<ref name="odonnell2020.com" />

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in New Jersey}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from New Jersey|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in New Jersey}}
Democrat [[Cory Booker]] won a second full term in office, having first won his seat in a 2013 special election after serving seven years as the mayor of [[Newark, New Jersey|Newark]]. He defeated Republican Rick Mehta by a margin of more than 16 percentage points.

Booker had sought his party's [[Democratic Party presidential primaries, 2020|nomination]] for [[President of the United States]] in [[2020 United States presidential election|2020]]. He suspended his presidential campaign on January 13, 2020, and confirmed his intention to seek a second Senate term.<ref name="Biryukov" />

Attorney Rik Mehta defeated engineer Hirsh Singh, 2018 Independent U.S. Senate candidate Tricia Flanagan, 2018 independent U.S. Senate candidate Natalie Lynn Rivera, and Eugene Anagnos for the Republican nomination.

Green Party candidate Madelyn Hoffman and two independent candidates also appeared on the general election ballot.

New Jersey has not elected a Republican senator since 1972, and all pundits expected Booker to be easily re-elected.<ref name="xTVKu" />

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in New Mexico}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from New Mexico|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in New Mexico}}

Two-term Democrat [[Tom Udall]] was the only incumbent Democratic U.S. senator retiring in 2020.<ref name="NMsen" /> Democratic U.S. representative [[Ben Ray Luján]]<ref name="pSmEQ" /> defeated Republican Mark Ronchetti by 6 percentage points.

Luján won the Democratic nomination without serious opposition.

Ronchetti, the former [[KRQE]] chief [[meteorologist]], defeated former U.S. Interior Department official Gavin Clarkson and executive director for the New Mexico Alliance for Life Elisa Martinez in the primary.<ref name="PJhvb" /><ref name="vwG6I" /><ref name="tSUQl" />

Libertarian Bob Walsh also appeared on the general election ballot.{{citation needed|date=October 2020}}

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in North Carolina}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from North Carolina|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in North Carolina}}

Republican [[Thom Tillis]] won a second term in office, defeating Democratic former state senator [[Cal Cunningham]].

Cunningham defeated [[North Carolina State Senate|state senator]] [[Erica D. Smith]] and [[Mecklenburg County, North Carolina|Mecklenburg County]] commissioner Trevor Fuller for the Democratic nomination. Tillis defeated three opponents.<ref name="PrimaryNight" />

The Libertarian Party and the Constitution Party had candidates on the general election ballot.

Despite having grown unpopular among both centrist and conservative Republicans due to his inconsistent support of Trump, and trailing narrowly in polls for almost the entire cycle,<ref name="North Carolina competitive" /> Tillis won re-election by nearly 2 points.<ref>[https://www.newsobserver.com/news/politics-government/election/article247097217.html Cunningham concedes to Tillis in North Carolina U.S. Senate race.] ''The News & Observer''. Retrieved November 10, 2020.</ref>

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Oklahoma}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Oklahoma|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Oklahoma}}

Republican [[Jim Inhofe]] easily won a fifth term in office, defeating Democrat [[2020 United States Senate election in Oklahoma|Abby Broyles]] by more than 30 percentage points.

Inhofe defeated J.J. Stitt, a farmer and gun shop owner, and Neil Mavis, a former Libertarian Party candidate, for the Republican nomination.<ref name="lWdzQ" />

Broyles, an attorney, defeated perennial candidate Sheila Bilyeu and 2018 [[Oklahoma's 5th congressional district|5th congressional district]] candidate Elysabeth Britt for the Democratic nomination.

Libertarian candidate Robert Murphy and two Independents also appeared on the general election ballot.

Oklahoma is one of the most solidly Republican states<ref name="ddhq-2016-pvi" /> and Inhofe won in a landslide.

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Oregon}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Oregon|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Oregon}}

Democrat [[Jeff Merkley]] won a third term in office, defeating Republican [[Jo Rae Perkins]] by more than 17 percentage points. Merkley also received the [[Oregon Independent Party]] and the [[Working Families Party]] nominations.<ref name="OR2020" />

Perkins, a 2014 U.S. Senate and 2018 U.S. House candidate, defeated three other candidates in the Republican primary with 49.29% of the vote. She is a supporter of [[QAnon]].<ref name="q93dT" />

Ibrahim Taher was also on the general election ballot, representing the [[Pacific Green Party]]<ref name="35VnK" /> and the [[Oregon Progressive Party]]. Gary Dye represented the Libertarian Party.<ref name="OR2020" />

== South Carolina ==
{{Infobox election

 | party1             = Republican Party (US)

 | map_image          = 2020 United States Senate election in South Carolina results map by county.svg

 | map_caption        = County results<br>'''Graham:''' {{legend0|#FFB2B2|40–50%}} {{legend0|#E27F7F|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#D72F30|70–80%}}<br />'''Harrison:''' {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}} {{legend0|#584cde|70–80%}}
 | title              = U.S. senator
 | before_election    = [[Lindsey Graham]]
 | before_party       = Republican Party (US)
 | after_election    = [[Lindsey Graham]]
 | after_party       = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in South Carolina}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from South Carolina|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in South Carolina}}

Three-term Republican [[Lindsey Graham]] won a fourth term in office, defeating Democrat [[Jaime Harrison]] by over ten percentage points in a highly publicized race.

Graham defeated three opponents in the June 9 Republican primary.<ref name="2Z7qK" />

After his primary opponents dropped out, former [[South Carolina Democratic Party]] chairman [[Jaime Harrison]] was unopposed for the Democratic nomination.

Bill Bledsoe won the [[Constitution Party (United States)|Constitution Party]] nomination. On October 1, 2020, Bledsoe dropped out of the race and endorsed Graham, but remained on the ballot as required by state law.<ref name="ZJaH0" />

Despite the significant Republican lean of the state as a whole, polls indicated that the Senate election was competitive, with summer polling ranging from a tie to a modest advantage for Graham.<ref name="QP7Jj" /><ref name="NV8J4" /> Graham's popularity had declined as a result of his close embrace of Trump, reversing his outspoken criticism of Trump in the 2016 campaign.<ref name="eIMxb" /><ref name="epgJn" />

Graham's victory was by a much larger margin than expected,<ref>[https://www.politico.com/news/2020/11/03/south-carolina-senate-election-results-2020-433581 Graham fends off Harrison in South Carolina Senate race], ''[[Politico]]'', November 3, 2020.</ref> as part of a broader pattern of Republicans overperforming polls in 2020.

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in South Dakota}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from South Dakota|2020 United States House of Representatives election in South Dakota}}

Republican [[Mike Rounds]], former [[governor of South Dakota]], won a second term in office, defeating Democrat [[Daniel Ahlers|Dan Ahlers]].

Rounds faced a primary challenge from [[South Dakota House of Representatives|state representative]] [[Scyller Borglum]].<ref name="borglum-announces" />

Ahlers, a South Dakota state representative, ran unopposed in the Democratic primary.<ref name="ahlers-announces" />

One independent candidate, Clayton Walker, filed but failed to qualify for the ballot.<ref name="sd-candidate-list" />

== Tennessee ==
{{Infobox election

 | map_image          = 2020 United States Senate election in Tennessee results map by county.svg
 | map_size           = 300px
 | map_caption        = County results<br>'''Hagerty:''' {{legend0|#E27F7F|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#D72F30|70–80%}} {{legend0|#C21B18|80–90%}} <br />'''Bradshaw:''' {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}}

 | after_party       = Republican Party (US)

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Tennessee}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Tennessee|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Tennessee}}

Three-term Republican [[Lamar Alexander]] was re-elected in 2014. He announced in December 2018 that he would not seek a fourth term.<ref name="rY7K2" />

Assisted by an endorsement from Trump,<ref name="U9GcJ" /> former [[ambassador to Japan]] [[Bill Hagerty]] won the Republican nomination<ref name="x3qCt" /> and the seat.

Hagerty defeated orthopedic surgeon [[Manny Sethi]]<ref name="cpkGs" /> and 13 others in the Republican primary.

Environmental activist [[Marquita Bradshaw]] of [[Memphis, Tennessee|Memphis]] defeated James Mackler, an [[Iraq War]] veteran and [[Nashville]] attorney,<ref name="N509z" /> in the Democratic primary, a major upset.

Nine independent candidates also appeared on the general election ballot.

Hagerty easily defeated Bradshaw.

== Texas ==
{{Infobox election
 | election_name      = Texas election
 | country            = Texas
 | type               = presidential
 | ongoing            = no
 | previous_election  = 2014 United States Senate election in Texas

 | next_year          = 2026
 <!-- Election date not needed if General AND same as main article's date-->
 | image_size         = x136px
 | image1             = John Cornyn (cropped).jpg
 | nominee1           = '''[[John Cornyn]]'''
 | party1             = Republican Party (US)
 | popular_vote1      = '''5,962,983'''
 | percentage1        = '''53.51%'''
 | image2             = MJHegar (cropped).jpg
 | nominee2           = [[MJ Hegar]]
 | party2             = Democratic Party (US)
 | popular_vote2      = 4,888,764
 | percentage2        = 43.87%
 | map_image          = 2020 United States Senate election in Texas results map by county.svg
 | map_size           = 300px
 | map_caption        = County results<br>'''Cornyn:''' {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}} {{legend0|#C21B18|80–90%}} {{legend0|#A80000|>90%}}<br>
'''Hegar:''' {{legend0|#a5b0ff|40–50%}} {{legend0|#7996e2|50–60%}} {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}}
 | title              = U.S. senator
 | before_election    = [[John Cornyn]]
 | before_party       = Republican Party (US)
 | after_election    = [[John Cornyn]]
 | after_party       = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Texas}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Texas|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Texas}}

Republican [[John Cornyn]] won a fourth<ref name="cornyn" /> term in office, defeating Democrat [[MJ Hegar]] by a little less than ten percentage points.

Cornyn defeated four other candidates in the Republican primary, with 76.04% of the vote.

Hegar, an [[United States Air Force|Air Force]] combat veteran and the 2018 Democratic nominee for Texas's 31st congressional district,<ref name="hegar-website" /> defeated runner-up [[Texas State Senate|state senator]] [[Royce West]] and 11 other candidates in the Democratic primary. Hegar and West advanced to a primary run-off election on July 14 to decide the nomination, and Hegar prevailed.

The Green and Libertarian Parties also appeared on the general election ballot. Candidates from the Human Rights Party and the People over Politics Party and three independents failed to qualify.

Statewide races in Texas have been growing more competitive in recent years, and polling in August/September showed Cornyn with a lead of 4–10 points over Hegar, with a significant fraction of the electorate still undecided.<ref name="rJfdK" /><ref name="raDk0" /> Cornyn's victory was at the higher end of the polling spectrum.

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Virginia}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Virginia|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in Virginia}}

Democrat [[Mark Warner]] won a third term in office, defeating Republican [[Daniel Gade]].

Warner ran unopposed in the Democratic primary.<ref name="MWarner" />

Gade, a professor and U.S. Army veteran,<ref name="Gade" /> defeated teacher Alissa Baldwin<ref name="ABaldwin" /> and U.S. Army veteran and intelligence officer Thomas Speciale<ref name="Speciale" /> in the Republican primary.

{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in West Virginia}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from West Virginia|2020 United States House of Representatives elections in West Virginia}}

Republican [[Shelley Moore Capito]] was re-elected to a second term in a landslide, defeating Democrat [[Paula Jean Swearengin]] by 43 points.

Capito was unsuccessfully challenged in the Republican primary by farmer Larry Butcher and Allen Whitt, president of the West Virginia Family Policy Council.<ref name="AWhitt" />

Swearengin, an environmental activist and unsuccessful candidate for Senate in [[2018 United States Senate election in West Virginia|2018]],<ref name="bustle-knock-down" /> won the Democratic primary, defeating former mayor of [[South Charleston, West Virginia|South Charleston]] Richie Robb and former [[West Virginia State Senate|state senator]] [[Richard Ojeda]], who previously ran for Congress and, briefly, president in 2020.

Libertarian candidate David Moran also appeared on the general election ballot.

== Wyoming ==
{{Infobox election
 | election_name      = Wyoming election
 | country            = Wyoming
 | type               = presidential
 | ongoing            = no
 | previous_election  = 2014 United States Senate election in Wyoming
 | previous_year      = 2014
 | next_election      = 2026 United States Senate election in Wyoming

 | image_size         = x136px
 | image1             = Cynthia_Lummis_U.S._Senator.jpg
 | nominee1           = '''[[Cynthia Lummis]]'''
 | party1             = Republican Party (US)
 | popular_vote1      = '''198,100'''
 | percentage1        = '''72.85%'''
 | image2             = Merav Ben-David (cropped).png
 | nominee2           = [[Merav Ben-David]]
 | party2             = Democratic Party (US)
 | popular_vote2      = 72,766
 | percentage2        = 26.76%
 | map_image          = 2020 United States Senate election in Wyoming results map by county.svg
 | map_size           = 250px
 | map_caption        = County results<br>'''Lummis:''' {{legend0|#e27f7f|50–60%}} {{legend0|#d75d5d|60–70%}} {{legend0|#d72f30|70–80%}} {{legend0|#c21b18|80–90%}} {{legend0|#a80000|>90%}}<br>'''Ben-David:''' {{legend0|#6674de|60–70%}}
 | title              = U.S. senator
 | before_election    = [[Mike Enzi]]
 | before_party       = Republican Party (US)
 | after_election     = [[Cynthia Lummis]]
 | after_party        = Republican Party (US)
}}
{{Main|2020 United States Senate election in Wyoming}}
{{See also|List of United States senators from Wyoming|2020 United States House of Representatives election in Wyoming}}

Four-term Republican [[Mike Enzi]] announced in May 2019 that he would retire. Republican nominee [[Cynthia Lummis]] defeated Democratic nominee [[Merav Ben-David]] by more than 46 percentage points.

Lummis won the Republican nomination in a field of nine candidates.<ref name="roll-call-lummis-running" />

Ben-David, the chair of the Department of Zoology and Physiology at the [[University of Wyoming]], defeated community activists Yana Ludwig and James Debrine, think-tank executive Nathan Wendt, and perennial candidates Rex Wilde and Kenneth R. Casner for the Democratic nomination.
